**New Orleans**[^cite_note-9] (commonly known as **NOLA** or **The Big Easy**, among other nicknames) is a [consolidated city-parish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Consolidated_city-county) located along the [Mississippi River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mississippi_River) in the U.S. state of [Louisiana](18130.md). With a population of 383,997 at the 2020 census, New Orleans is the [most populous city in Louisiana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_municipalities_in_Louisiana), the second-most populous in the [Deep South](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deep_South) (after [Atlanta](3138.md)), and the twelfth-most populous in the [Southeastern United States](816925.md);[^cite_note-\:2-10][^cite_note-henrylouismencken-11] the [New Orleans metropolitan area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Orleans_metropolitan_area), with about 1 million residents, is the [59th-most populous metropolitan area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_metropolitan_statistical_areas) in the United States.[^cite_note-PopEstCBSA-12] New Orleans serves as a major port and commercial hub for the broader [Gulf Coast region](11969.md). The city is coextensive with [Orleans Parish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orleans_Parish,_Louisiana).[^cite_note-coextensive-13]

New Orleans is renowned for [its distinctive music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Music_of_New_Orleans), [Creole cuisine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louisiana_Creole_cuisine), [unique dialects](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Orleans_English), and its annual celebrations and festivals, most notably [Mardi Gras](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mardi_Gras_in_New_Orleans). The historic heart of the city is the [French Quarter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_Quarter), known for its [French and Spanish Creole architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_architecture#America) and vibrant [nightlife](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nightlife) along [Bourbon Street](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bourbon_Street).

Founded in 1718 by French colonists, New Orleans was once the territorial capital of [French Louisiana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louisiana_\(New_France\)) before becoming part of the United States in the [Louisiana Purchase](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louisiana_Purchase) of 1803. New Orleans in 1840 was the third most populous city in the United States,[^cite_note-14] and it was the largest city in the [American South](179553.md) from the [Antebellum era](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antebellum_South) until after [World War II](32927.md). The city has historically been very vulnerable to [flooding](50482.md), due to its high rainfall, low-lying elevation, poor natural drainage, and proximity to multiple bodies of water. State and federal authorities have installed [a complex system of levees and drainage pumps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Drainage_in_New_Orleans) in an effort to protect the city.[^cite_note-15][^cite_note-16]

New Orleans was [severely affected](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Effects_of_Hurricane_Katrina_in_New_Orleans) by [Hurricane Katrina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hurricane_Katrina) in late August 2005, which flooded more than 80% of the city, killed more than 1,300 people, and displaced thousands of residents, causing a [population decline](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Orleans_diaspora) of over 50%.[^cite_note-17] Since Katrina, major [redevelopment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urban_renewal) efforts have led to a rebound in the city's population. Concerns have been expressed about [gentrification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gentrification) and consequent displacement.[^cite_note-18][^cite_note-19][^cite_note-20][^cite_note-21]

## Etymology and nicknames

Before the arrival of European colonists, the indigenous [Choctaw](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Choctaw) people called the area of present-day New Orleans *Bulbancha*, which translates as "land of many tongues".[^cite_note-22][^cite_note-23] It appears to have been a contraction of *balbáha a̱shah*, which means "there are foreign speakers". In his book *Histoire de la Louisiane*, [Antoine-Simon Le Page du Pratz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antoine-Simon_Le_Page_du_Pratz) wrote that the indigenous name referred to the [Mississippi River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mississippi_River) and that the use of the same name for the settlement relates to Native American concepts of the close interaction between rivers and their surrounding land.[^cite_note-24]

The name of New Orleans derives from the original French name, *La Nouvelle-Orléans*, which was given to the city in honor of [Philippe II, Duke of Orléans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippe_II,_Duke_of_Orl%C3%A9ans), who served as [Louis XV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louis_XV)'s regent from 1715 to 1723.[^cite_note-\:0-25] The French city of [Orléans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orl%C3%A9ans) itself was originally known as *civitas Aurelianorum*, possibly named after one of the many Roman Emperors bearing the *nomen gentilicum* around that time, which later evolved into Orléans.[^cite_note-26]

Following the defeat in the [Seven Years' War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seven_Years'_War), France formally transferred the possession of [Louisiana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louisiana_\(New_France\)) to [Spain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Spain_\(1700%E2%80%931808\)), with which France had secretly signed the [Treaty of Fontainebleau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Fontainebleau_\(1762\)) a year earlier, in the [Treaty of Paris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Paris_\(1763\)) of 1763. The Spanish renamed the city *Nueva Orleans* (pronounced [\[ˌnweβa oɾleˈans\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:IPA/Spanish)), which was used until 1800.[^cite_note-27] The United States, which had [acquired possession](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louisiana_Purchase) from [France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_First_Republic) in 1803, anglicized the French name to *New Orleans*.

New Orleans has several nicknames, including these:

- *Crescent City*, alluding to the course of the [Lower Mississippi River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lower_Mississippi_River) around and through the city.

  [^cite_note-NOCVBnicknames-28]

- *The Big Easy*, possibly a reference by musicians in the early 20th century to the relative ease of finding work there.

  [^cite_note-29]

  [^cite_note-\:1-30]

- *The City that Care Forgot*, used since at least 1938,

  [^cite_note-31]

  &#x20;referring to the outwardly easygoing, carefree nature of the residents.

  [^cite_note-\:1-30]

- *NOLA*, the acronym for New Orleans, Louisiana.

## History

Main article: [History of New Orleans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_New_Orleans)

For a chronological guide, see [Timeline of New Orleans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_New_Orleans).

### French–Spanish colonial era

Main articles: [Louisiana (New France)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louisiana_\(New_France\)), [New France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_France), [Treaty of Paris (1763)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Paris_\(1763\)), [Louisiana (New Spain)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louisiana_\(New_Spain\)), and [New Spain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Spain)

See also: [Seven Years' War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seven_Years'_War), [French and Indian War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_and_Indian_War), [Gulf Coast campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gulf_Coast_campaign), [Spain and the American Revolutionary War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spain_and_the_American_Revolutionary_War), [Third Treaty of San Ildefonso](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Third_Treaty_of_San_Ildefonso), and [Treaty of Aranjuez (1801)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Aranjuez_\(1801\))

Historical claims

>  [Kingdom of France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_France) 1718–1762\
> &#x20; [Spanish Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_Empire) 1762–1801\
> &#x20; [First French Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_French_Empire) 1801–1803\
> &#x20; [United States](3434750.md) 1803–present

*La Nouvelle-Orléans* (New Orleans) was founded in the spring of 1718 by the French [Mississippi Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mississippi_Company) under [Jean-Baptiste Le Moyne de Bienville](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jean-Baptiste_Le_Moyne_de_Bienville), on land traditionally inhabited by the [Chitimacha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chitimacha) people.[^cite_note-32] The city was named for [Philippe II, Duke of Orléans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippe_II,_Duke_of_Orl%C3%A9ans), then-regent of the [Kingdom of France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_France) whose title derived from the French city of [Orléans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orl%C3%A9ans).[^cite_note-\:0-25] As a colony, [French Louisiana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_Louisiana) faced conflict with [Native American tribes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Native_Americans_in_the_United_States) navigating rival European powers. In 1729, the [Natchez revolt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natchez_revolt) erupted with an attack on [Fort Rosalie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fort_Rosalie), resulting in the deaths of over 200 French colonists.[^cite_note-33] Governor [Étienne Perier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C3%89tienne_Perier_\(governor\)) launched a retaliatory campaign that effectively destroyed the [Natchez people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natchez_people), but it soured relations between France and the territory's Native Americans leading directly into the [Chickasaw Wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chickasaw_Wars) of the 1730s.[^cite_note-34][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGayarré1854450-35]

Native resistance continued into the 1740s under governor [Pierre de Rigaud, marquis de Vaudreuil-Cavagnial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pierre_de_Rigaud,_marquis_de_Vaudreuil-Cavagnial), as tribes including the [Chickasaw](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chickasaw) and [Choctaw](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Choctaw) leveraged competing colonial interests.[^cite_note-36] Raids intensified as French economic instability weakened colonial defenses, with some Chickasaw attacks reaching as far south as [Baton Rouge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baton_Rouge). Meanwhile, labor shortages led the French colonists to turn to the [Atlantic slave trade](49005.md). By the early 1720s, [enslaved Africans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slavery_in_the_colonial_United_States) were arriving in significant numbers, and in 1724, the [Code Noir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Code_Noir) formalized harsh laws governing their lives.[^cite_note-37] A distinct Afro-Creole culture began to develop, blending African traditions with Catholicism and French language, giving rise to practices such as [Louisiana Voodoo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louisiana_Voodoo) and the [Louisiana Creole](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louisiana_Creole) language.[^cite_note-38][^cite_note-39]



1724 plan for Saint Louis Parish Church, New Orleans, Louisiana, by [Adrien de Pauger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adrien_de_Pauger)

New Orleans quickly emerged as a cultural and commercial hub in French Louisiana. Its position as a key port made it the gateway for goods moving between the interior of North America and the Atlantic world. Institutions like the [Ursuline sisters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_Ursulines_in_New_Orleans), founded in 1727 by nuns sponsored by the [Company of the Indies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Company_of_the_Indies), reflected the city's integration into French religious and educational networks.[^cite_note-40] The convent educated girls and remains foundational to several modern schools in the city. Early city planning and architecture were shaped by military engineers like Pierre Le Blond de Tour and [Adrien de Pauger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adrien_de_Pauger), whose designs laid out the enduring street grid and fortifications.[^cite_note-41] By the 1740s, public works programs under engineer Ignace François Broutin transformed the city's architecture, blending colonial governance with a distinct Creole character.

After France ceded Louisiana to the [Spanish Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_colonization_of_the_Americas) in the [1763 Treaty of Paris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Paris_\(1763\)), New Orleans residents resisted Spanish rule. Local residents staged the [Louisiana Rebellion of 1768](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louisiana_Rebellion_of_1768), briefly seizing control of the city and sending a delegation to France to appeal for renewed French authority.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECumminsKheher_SchaferHaasKurtz201470-42] Their efforts failed, and King [Louis XV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louis_XV) reaffirmed Spanish sovereignty.[^cite_note-Presas-43] Nearly all of the surviving 18th-century [architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Architecture) of the Vieux Carré ([French Quarter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_Quarter)) dates from the Spanish period, notably excepting the [Old Ursuline Convent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_Ursuline_Convent,_New_Orleans).[^cite_note-44] During the [American Revolutionary War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Revolutionary_War), New Orleans played a key role as a supply hub for the American cause, particularly under Spanish governor [Bernardo de Gálvez y Madrid, Count of Gálvez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bernardo_de_G%C3%A1lvez_y_Madrid,_Count_of_G%C3%A1lvez), who led a campaign against the British from the city in 1779.[^cite_note-45] From the 1760s onward, [Filipinos](2257094.md) also began settling in the region.[^cite_note-46]

### United States territorial era

Main articles: [Louisiana Purchase](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louisiana_Purchase), [Territory of Orleans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Territory_of_Orleans), and [Dominican Creoles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dominican_Creoles)



Flag raising in the [Place d'Armes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jackson_Square_\(New_Orleans\)) (now Jackson Square), New Orleans, after the [Louisiana Purchase](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louisiana_Purchase), marking the transfer of sovereignty over French Louisiana to the United States, December 20, 1803

The [Third Treaty of San Ildefonso](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Third_Treaty_of_San_Ildefonso) in 1800 restored [French](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_First_Republic) control of New Orleans and Louisiana, but [Napoleon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Napoleon_I_of_France) sold both to the United States in the [Louisiana Purchase](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louisiana_Purchase) in 1803.[^cite_note-47] Thereafter, the city grew rapidly with influxes of Americans, [French](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_people), [Creoles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louisiana_Creole_people) and [Africans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demographics_of_Africa). Later immigrants were [Irish](775859.md), [Germans](152735.md), [Poles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polish_people) and [Italians](385155.md). Major [commodity crops](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commodity_crops) of [sugar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sugar_cane) and [cotton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cotton) were cultivated with [slave](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slavery_in_the_United_States) labor on nearby large [plantations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plantations_in_the_American_South).

Between 1791 and 1810, thousands of [St. Dominican](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint_Dominicans) [refugees](45547.md) from the [Haitian Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haitian_Revolution), both [whites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_people) and [free people of color](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_people_of_color) (*affranchis* or *gens de couleur libres*), arrived in New Orleans; a number brought their slaves with them, many of whom were native Africans or of full-blood descent.[^cite_note-Lachance1988-48] While Governor [Claiborne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_C.C._Claiborne) and other officials wanted to keep out additional [free black](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_negro) people, the French Creoles wanted to increase the French-speaking population. In addition to bolstering the territory's French-speaking population, these refugees had a significant impact on the culture of Louisiana, including developing its sugar industry and cultural institutions.[^cite_note-49]

As more refugees were allowed into the [Territory of Orleans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Territory_of_Orleans), [St. Dominican](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint_Dominicans) refugees who had first gone to [Cuba](5042481.md) also arrived.[^cite_note-AAM-50] Many of the white [Francophones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geographical_distribution_of_French_speakers) had been deported by officials in Cuba in 1809 as retaliation for [Bonapartist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bonapartist) schemes.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGitlin200954-51] Nearly 90 percent of these immigrants settled in New Orleans. The 1809 migration brought 2,731 whites, 3,102 free people of color (of [mixed-race](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mixed-race) European and African descent), and 3,226 slaves of primarily African descent, doubling the city's population. The city became 63 percent black, a greater proportion than [Charleston, South Carolina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charleston,_South_Carolina)'s 53 percent at that time.[^cite_note-AAM-50]

#### Slave rebellion

On January 8–11, 1811, about 500 enslaved Africans in St. Charles and St. John the Baptist parishes rose up in the [German Coast rebellion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1811_German_Coast_uprising) against their enslavers, killing two white men in the process. They proceeded to march south toward New Orleans and were eventually controlled by the local militia, with numerous casualties on both sides. The uprising has been called the "largest slave rebellion in US history."[^cite_note-52]

### Battle of New Orleans and antebellum period

Main articles: [Battle of New Orleans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_New_Orleans) and [War of 1812](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_of_1812)



The [Battle of New Orleans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_New_Orleans)

Plan of the city and suburbs of New Orleans : from an actual survey made in 1815

Plan of the city and suburbs of New Orleans: from a survey made in 1815

[^cite_note-53]

During the final campaign of the [War of 1812](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_of_1812), the British sent a force of 11,000 in an attempt to capture New Orleans. Despite great challenges, General [Andrew Jackson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andrew_Jackson), with support from the U.S. Navy, successfully cobbled together a force of [militia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Militia) from Louisiana and [Mississippi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mississippi_Territory), U.S. Army regulars, a large contingent of [Tennessee](30395.md) state militia, [Kentucky](16846.md) frontiersmen and local [privateers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Privateer) (the latter led by the pirate [Jean Lafitte](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jean_Lafitte)), to decisively defeat the British in the [Battle of New Orleans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_New_Orleans) on January 8, 1815.[^cite_note-Groom2007-54] The armies had not learned of the [Treaty of Ghent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Ghent), which had been signed on December 24, 1814 (although not ratified by the U.S. government until February 16, 1815). The fighting in Louisiana began in December 1814 and did not end until late January, after the Americans held off the Royal Navy during a ten-day [siege of Fort St. Philip](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siege_of_Fort_St._Philip_\(1815\)).[^cite_note-Groom2007-54]



Mississippi River [steamboats](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steamboats_of_the_Mississippi) at New Orleans, 1853

As a [port](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port), New Orleans played a major role during the [antebellum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antebellum_South) period in the [Atlantic slave trade](49005.md). The port handled commodities for export from the interior and imported goods from other countries, which were warehoused and transferred in New Orleans to smaller vessels and distributed along the Mississippi River watershed. The river was filled with steamboats, flatboats and sailing ships. Despite its role in the [slave trade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_slavery), New Orleans at the time also had the largest and most prosperous community of free persons of color in the nation, who were often educated, middle-class property owners.[^cite_note-pbsjazz-55][^cite_note-56]



Sale of Estates, Pictures and Slaves in the Rotunda at New Orleans, 1842

[^cite_note-57]

New Orleans housed the largest slave market in the country, particularly after the U.S. ended the international slave trade in 1808. The domestic trade surged, with two-thirds of more than a million enslaved people forcibly relocated to the [Deep South](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deep_South). The trade's economic value was immense as slaves were collectively valued at half a billion dollars, and the broader economy surrounding the trade, including transport and services, generated billions more. As a result, New Orleans benefited significantly, both financially and commercially, from this system.[^cite_note-58]

Following the Louisiana Purchase, Anglo-Americans and later German and Irish immigrants migrated to the city, contributing to its rapid growth.[^cite_note-manumission-59] By 1840, New Orleans was the wealthiest and third-most populous city in the U.S.[^cite_note-60] The white Francophone population remained influential, with French still used in some schools.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGitlin2009166-61] Free people of color (*gens de couleur libres*), mostly of mixed race and largely Francophone, formed a distinct artisan and professional class, even as the majority of black residents remained enslaved.[^cite_note-manumission-59] The city's prosperity was shadowed by repeated [epidemics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_epidemics_and_pandemics) of [yellow fever](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yellow_fever) and other tropical and [infectious diseases](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Infection), which killed over 150,000 residents in the 19th century.[^cite_note-62][^cite_note-63] By 1860, the city's population had reached nearly 170,000, its per capita income was the second highest in the nation, and it was the third-largest U.S. port by import tonnage.[^cite_note-nystrom-64]

### Civil War–Reconstruction era

See also: [New Orleans in the American Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Orleans_in_the_American_Civil_War) and [Louisiana in the American Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louisiana_in_the_American_Civil_War)



The starving people of New Orleans under Union occupation during the Civil War, 1862

As the Creole elite feared, the [American Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Civil_War) changed their world. In April 1862, following the city's occupation by the Union Navy after the [Battle of Forts Jackson and St. Philip](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Forts_Jackson_and_St._Philip), [Gen. Benjamin F. Butler](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benjamin_Butler_\(politician\)) – a respected Massachusetts lawyer serving in that state's militia – was appointed military governor. New Orleans residents supportive of the Confederacy nicknamed him "Beast" Butler, because of an order he issued. After his troops had been assaulted and harassed in the streets by women still loyal to the Confederate cause, his order warned that such future occurrences would result in his men treating such women as those "plying their avocation in the streets", implying that they would treat the women like prostitutes. Accounts of this spread widely. He also came to be called "Spoons" Butler because of the alleged looting that his troops did while occupying the city, during which time he himself supposedly pilfered silver flatware.[^cite_note-65]

Significantly, Butler abolished French-language instruction in city schools. Statewide measures in 1864 and, after the war, 1868 further strengthened the English-only policy imposed by federal representatives. With the predominance of English speakers, that language had already become dominant in business and government.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGitlin2009166-61] By the end of the 19th century, French usage had faded. It was also under pressure from Irish, Italian and German immigrants.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGitlin2009180-66] However, as late as 1902 "one-fourth of the population of the city spoke French in ordinary daily intercourse, while another two-fourths was able to understand the language perfectly,"[^cite_note-67] and as late as 1945, many elderly Creole women spoke no English.[^cite_note-68] The last major French language newspaper, *[L'Abeille de la Nouvelle-Orléans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/L'Abeille_de_la_Nouvelle-Orl%C3%A9ans)* (New Orleans Bee), ceased publication on December 27, 1923, after 96 years.[^cite_note-Brasseaux2005-69] According to some sources, *Le Courrier de la Nouvelle Orléans* continued until 1955.[^cite_note-70]

As the city was captured and occupied early in the war, it was spared the destruction through warfare suffered by many other cities of the [American South](179553.md). The [Union Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Union_Army) eventually extended its control north along the [Mississippi River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mississippi_River) and along the coastal areas. As a result, most of the southern portion of Louisiana was originally exempted from the liberating provisions of the 1863 [Emancipation Proclamation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emancipation_Proclamation) issued by President [Abraham Lincoln](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abraham_Lincoln). Large numbers of rural ex-slaves and some free people of color from the city volunteered for the first regiments of Black troops in the War. Led by Brigadier General [Daniel Ullman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daniel_Ullman) (1810–1892), of the 78th Regiment of New York State Volunteers Militia, they were known as the "Corps d'Afrique". While that name had been used by a militia before the war, that group was composed of [free people of color](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_people_of_color). The new group was made up mostly of former slaves. They were supplemented in the last two years of the War by newly organized [United States Colored Troops](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Colored_Troops), who played an increasingly important part in the war.[^cite_note-71]



Black and white [dockworkers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Orleans_dock_workers_and_unionization) resting on cotton bales

Violence in the South, including the [Memphis Riots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Memphis_Riots_of_1866) and [New Orleans Riot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Orleans_Riot) of 1866, spurred Congress to pass the [Reconstruction Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reconstruction_Act) and [Fourteenth Amendment](31666.md), granting citizenship and civil rights to freedmen and free people of color. During Reconstruction, Louisiana and Texas were governed under the [Fifth Military District](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fifth_Military_District), and Louisiana was readmitted to the Union in 1868 with a new constitution that established [universal male suffrage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Universal_manhood_suffrage), universal public education, and elected both black and white officials. [P.B.S. Pinchback](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/P.B.S._Pinchback) briefly served as Louisiana's Republican governor in 1872, becoming the first U.S. governor of African descent. New Orleans also maintained a racially integrated [public school system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Orleans_Public_Schools) during this period. However, wartime destruction, a financial recession, and the Panic of 1873 hindered economic recovery. From 1868, white insurgents used violence to suppress Black voters and disrupt Republican gatherings, culminating in the 1872 contested gubernatorial election and the rise of the "[White League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_League)", a paramilitary group supporting Democrats. In 1874, they seized state offices during the [Battle of Liberty Place](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Liberty_Place), and by 1876, [Redeemers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Redeemers) had reclaimed the state legislature. Federal troops withdrew in 1877, ending [Reconstruction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reconstruction_Era). In 1892 the racially integrated unions of New Orleans led a [general strike in the city](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1892_New_Orleans_general_strike) from November 8 to 12, shutting down the city & winning the vast majority of their demands.[^cite_note-72][^cite_note-73]

### Jim Crow era

[Dixiecrats](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dixiecrats) and [Democrats](5043544.md) passed [Jim Crow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jim_Crow) laws, establishing [racial segregation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Racial_segregation) in public facilities. In 1889, the legislature passed a [constitutional amendment](261586.md) incorporating a "[grandfather clause](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grandfather_clause)" that effectively [disfranchised](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Disfranchisement_after_Reconstruction_era) freedmen as well as the propertied people of color [manumitted](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manumission) before the war. Unable to vote, African Americans could not serve on juries or in local office, and were closed out of formal politics for generations. The Southern U.S. was ruled by a white Democratic Party. Public schools were racially segregated and remained so until 1960.

New Orleans's large community of well-educated, often French-speaking [free persons of color](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_persons_of_color) (*gens de couleur libres*), who had been free prior to the Civil War, fought against Jim Crow. They organized the *Comité des Citoyens* (Citizens Committee) to work for civil rights. As part of their legal campaign, they recruited one of their own, [Homer Plessy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Homer_Plessy), to test whether Louisiana's newly enacted Separate Car Act was constitutional. Plessy boarded a commuter train departing New Orleans for [Covington, Louisiana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Covington,_Louisiana), sat in the car reserved for whites only, and was arrested. The case resulting from this incident, *[Plessy v. Ferguson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plessy_v._Ferguson)*, was heard by the [U.S. Supreme Court](31737.md) in 1896. The court ruled that "[separate but equal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Separate_but_equal)" accommodations were constitutional, effectively upholding Jim Crow measures.

In practice, African-American public schools and facilities were underfunded across the South. The Supreme Court ruling contributed to this period as the [nadir of race relations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nadir_of_American_race_relations) in the United States. The rate of [lynchings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States) of black men was high across the South, as other states also disfranchised blacks and sought to impose Jim Crow. Nativist prejudices also surfaced. [Anti-Italian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-Italianism#Anti-Italianism_in_the_United_States) sentiment in 1891 contributed to the [lynchings of 11 Italians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/March_14,_1891_lynchings), some of whom had been acquitted of the murder of the police chief. Some were shot and killed in the jail where they were detained. It was the largest mass lynching in U.S. history.[^cite_note-74][^cite_note-Gambino2000-75] In July 1900 the city was swept by white mobs rioting after Robert Charles, a young African American, killed a policeman and temporarily escaped. The mob killed him and an estimated 20 other blacks; seven whites died in the days-long conflict, until a [state militia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Militia_\(United_States\)) suppressed it.

### 20th century



[Esplanade Avenue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Esplanade_Avenue,_New_Orleans) at Burgundy Street, looking lakewards (north) towards [Lake Pontchartrain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lake_Pontchartrain) in 1900



1943 waiting line at wartime Rationing Board office in New Orleans

New Orleans's economic and population zenith in relation to other American cities occurred in the antebellum period. It was the nation's fifth-largest city in 1860 (after New York, [Philadelphia](50585.md), [Boston](24437894.md) and Baltimore) and was significantly larger than all other southern cities.[^cite_note-Lewis-2003–175-76] From the mid-19th century onward rapid economic growth shifted to other areas, while New Orleans's relative importance steadily declined. The growth of railways and highways decreased river traffic, diverting goods to other transportation corridors and markets.[^cite_note-Lewis-2003–175-76] Thousands of the most ambitious [people of color](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Person_of_color) left the state in the [Great Migration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Migration_\(African_American\)) around [World War II](32927.md) and after, many for [West Coast](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Coast_of_the_United_States) destinations. From the late 1800s, most censuses recorded New Orleans slipping down the ranks in the list of largest American cities (New Orleans's population still continued to increase throughout the period, but at a slower rate than before the Civil War).

In 1929, a [streetcar strike](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1929_New_Orleans_streetcar_strike) took place in the city, during which serious unrest occurred.[^cite_note-77] It is also credited for the creation of the distinctly Louisianan [po' boy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Po'_boy) sandwich.[^cite_note-78][^cite_note-79]

By the mid-20th century, New Orleanians recognized that their city was no longer the leading urban area in the South. By 1950, [Houston](13774.md), [Dallas](53838.md), and [Atlanta](3138.md) exceeded New Orleans in size, and in 1960 [Miami](53846.md) eclipsed New Orleans, even as the latter's population reached its historic peak.[^cite_note-Lewis-2003–175-76] As with other older American cities, highway construction and suburban development drew residents from the center city to newer housing outside. The 1970 census recorded the first absolute decline in population since the city became part of the United States in 1803. The [New Orleans metropolitan area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Orleans_metropolitan_area) continued expanding in population, albeit more slowly than other major [Sun Belt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sun_Belt) cities. While the [Port of New Orleans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_of_New_Orleans) remained one of the nation's largest, automation and [containerization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Containerization) cost many jobs. The city's former role as banker to the South was supplanted by larger peer cities. New Orleans's economy had always been based more on trade and financial services than on manufacturing, but the city's relatively small manufacturing sector also shrank after World War II. Despite some economic development successes under the administrations of [deLesseps Story Morrison](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/DeLesseps_Story_Morrison) (1946–1961) and [Victor H. Schiro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Victor_H._Schiro) (1961–1970), metropolitan New Orleans's growth rate consistently lagged behind more vigorous cities.

During the later years of Mayor deLesseps Morrison's administration and throughout Victor Schiro's tenure, New Orleans became a focal point of the [Civil Rights Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civil_Rights_Movement). The [Southern Christian Leadership Conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Christian_Leadership_Conference) was founded in the city, and lunch counter sit-ins took place in [Canal Street](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canal_Street,_New_Orleans) department stores. Tensions escalated in 1960 during school desegregation following the *[Brown v. Board of Education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brown_v._Board_of_Education)* (1954) ruling. Six-year-old [Ruby Bridges](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ruby_Bridges) integrated [William Frantz Elementary School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Frantz_Elementary_School), becoming the first child of color to attend a previously all-white Southern school. Racial controversy also surrounded the [1956 Sugar Bowl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1956_Sugar_Bowl), when Georgia governor [Marvin Griffin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marvin_Griffin) opposed the participation of [Pitt Panthers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1955_Pittsburgh_Panthers_football_team) African-American fullback [Bobby Grier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bobby_Grier_\(Pittsburgh_Panthers\)).[^cite_note-fcflu-80][^cite_note-Mulé-81][^cite_note-82][^cite_note-83] Georgia Institute of Technology president [Blake R. Van Leer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blake_R._Van_Leer) defied the governor, and the game proceeded.[^cite_note-84] The federal [Civil Rights Act of 1964](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civil_Rights_Act_of_1964) and the [Voting Rights Act of 1965](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Voting_Rights_Act_of_1965) restored key constitutional protections, but economic and educational disparities between Black and White residents remained.[^cite_note-85][^cite_note-ReferenceA-86] As more affluent residents left the city, its population became increasingly poor and predominantly African-American. Beginning in 1980, Black-majority leadership emerged, working to address entrenched socioeconomic inequities.

By the late 20th century, New Orleans had grown increasingly reliant on tourism amid rising poverty, low educational attainment, and high crime, which hindered its adaptation to the broader U.S. shift toward a post-industrial service economy.[^cite_note-ReferenceA-86] Meanwhile, city leaders pursued geographic expansion through [ambitious drainage efforts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Drainage_in_New_Orleans). Engineer [A. Baldwin Wood](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A._Baldwin_Wood) designed a pump system that allowed development in formerly uninhabitable swamp and marsh areas, but over time, these areas subsided significantly below sea level.[^cite_note-87][^cite_note-88] Although the city had always faced flooding risks, awareness of its vulnerability grew after [Hurricane Betsy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hurricane_Betsy) in 1965 and the [May 8th 1995 Louisiana Flood](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/May_8th_1995_Louisiana_Flood). These events exposed the limits of the drainage system, prompting upgrades. By the 1980s and 1990s, scientists warned that [erosion of the marshlands and swamp surrounding New Orleans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coastal_erosion_in_Louisiana), exacerbated by developments like the [Mississippi River–Gulf Outlet Canal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mississippi_River%E2%80%93Gulf_Outlet_Canal), had left the city more exposed than ever to hurricane [storm surges](449744.md).

### 21st century

See also: [Effects of Hurricane Katrina in New Orleans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Effects_of_Hurricane_Katrina_in_New_Orleans) and [Reconstruction of New Orleans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reconstruction_of_New_Orleans)



[Hurricane Katrina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hurricane_Katrina) at its landfall near the Louisiana-Mississippi border

New Orleans was catastrophically affected by what Raymond B. Seed called "the worst engineering disaster in the world since [Chernobyl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chernobyl_disaster)", when [the federal levee system failed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2005_levee_failures_in_Greater_New_Orleans) during [Hurricane Katrina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hurricane_Katrina) on August 29, 2005.[^cite_note-Baker-89] By the time the hurricane approached the city on August 29, 2005, most residents had evacuated. As the hurricane passed through the [Gulf Coast region](11969.md) that day, the city's [federal flood protection](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flood_Control_Act_of_1965) system failed, resulting in the worst [civil engineering](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civil_engineering) disaster in American history at the time.[^cite_note-90] Floodwalls and [levees](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Levee) constructed by the [United States Army Corps of Engineers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Army_Corps_of_Engineers) failed below design specifications and 80% of the city flooded. Tens of thousands of residents who had remained were rescued or otherwise made their way to shelters of last resort at the [Louisiana Superdome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mercedes-Benz_Superdome) or the [New Orleans Morial Convention Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Orleans_Morial_Convention_Center). More than 1,500 people were recorded as having died in Louisiana, most in New Orleans, while others remain unaccounted for.[^cite_note-91][^cite_note-184_Katrina1-92] Before Hurricane Katrina, the city called for the first mandatory evacuation in its history, to be followed by another mandatory evacuation three years later with [Hurricane Gustav](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hurricane_Gustav).[^cite_note-93]



An aerial view from a United States Navy helicopter showing floodwaters around the Louisiana Superdome (stadium) and surrounding area (2005)

The city was declared off-limits to residents while efforts to clean up after [Hurricane Katrina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hurricane_Katrina) began. The approach of [Hurricane Rita](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hurricane_Rita) in September 2005 caused repopulation efforts to be postponed,[^cite_note-94] and the [Lower Ninth Ward](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lower_Ninth_Ward) was reflooded by Rita's storm surge.[^cite_note-184_Katrina1-92] Because of the scale of damage, many people resettled permanently outside the area. Federal, state, and local efforts supported recovery and rebuilding in severely damaged neighborhoods. The U.S. Census Bureau in July 2006 estimated the population to be 223,000; the city was estimated to have regained approximately 60% of its pre-Katrina population by summer 2007.[^cite_note-95] Ten years after the hurricane, the population had recovered to 80% of what it was at the 2000 census.[^cite_note-96]

Several major tourist events and other forms of revenue for the city have returned. Large conventions returned.[^cite_note-97][^cite_note-98] College bowl games returned for the [2006–2007 season](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2006%E2%80%9307_NCAA_football_bowl_games). The [New Orleans Saints](21720.md) returned that season. The [New Orleans Hornets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Orleans_Hornets) (now named the Pelicans) returned to the city for the [2007–2008 season](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2007%E2%80%9308_New_Orleans_Hornets_season). New Orleans hosted the [2008 NBA All-Star Game](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008_NBA_All-Star_Game) in addition to [Super Bowl XLVII](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Super_Bowl_XLVII). Major annual events such as [Mardi Gras](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Orleans_Mardi_Gras), [Voodoo Experience](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Voodoo_Experience), and the [Jazz & Heritage Festival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Orleans_Jazz_&_Heritage_Festival) were never displaced or canceled. A new annual festival, "The Running of the Bulls New Orleans", was created in 2007.[^cite_note-99]

On August 29, 2021, coincidentally the 16th anniversary of the landfall of Hurricane Katrina, [Hurricane Ida](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hurricane_Ida), a category 4 hurricane, made landfall near [Port Fourchon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_Fourchon,_Louisiana), where the [Hurricane Ida tornado outbreak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hurricane_Ida_tornado_outbreak) caused damage.[^cite_note-100] On January 1, 2025, [a truck attack](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_New_Orleans_truck_attack) occurred in New Orleans, killing 15 people and injuring 35. The attack was carried out as an act of [domestic terrorism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Domestic_terrorism) and was committed by Shamsud-Din Jabbar.

On December 30, 2025, 350 [Louisiana National Guard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louisiana_National_Guard) troops were deployed to New Orleans as part of a wave of [recent](80838047.md) nationwide [National Guard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Guard) deployments.[^cite_note-101][^cite_note-102][^cite_note-103]

## Geography



A true-color image captured by [ESA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ESA)'s [Sentinel-2A](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sentinel-2A) in April 2024, New Orleans positioned at the bottom right of the image. [Lake Pontchartrain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lake_Pontchartrain) prominently occupies the central area of the image, while the [Mississippi River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mississippi_River) can also be observed coursing through the city.

New Orleans is located in the [Mississippi River Delta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mississippi_River_Delta), south of [Lake Pontchartrain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lake_Pontchartrain), on the banks of the [Mississippi River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mississippi_River), approximately 105 miles (169 km) upriver from the [Gulf of Mexico](21076367.md). According to the [U.S. Census Bureau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._Census_Bureau), the city's area is 350 square miles (910 km<sup>2</sup>), of which 169 square miles (440 km<sup>2</sup>) is land and 181 square miles (470 km<sup>2</sup>) (52%) is water.[^cite_note-104] The area along the river is characterized by ridges and hollows.

### Elevation

See also: [Drainage in New Orleans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Drainage_in_New_Orleans)



Vertical cross-section, showing maximum levee height of 23 feet (7.0 m). Note that the depicted water level of the river equates to its average annual high-water mark, some fourteen feet above sea level; by contrast, the lake level exhibits little meaningful seasonal variation.

New Orleans was originally settled on the river's natural [levees](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Levee) or high ground. After the [Flood Control Act of 1965](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flood_Control_Act_of_1965), the [U.S. Army Corps of Engineers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Army_Corps_of_Engineers) built floodwalls and man-made levees around a much larger geographic footprint that included previous marshland and swamp. Over time, pumping of water from marshland allowed for development into lower elevation areas. Today, half of the city is at or below local mean sea level, while the other half is slightly above sea level. Evidence suggests that portions of the city may be dropping in elevation due to [subsidence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Subsidence).[^cite_note-105]

A 2007 study by [Tulane](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tulane_University) and [Xavier University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xavier_University_of_Louisiana) suggested that "51%... of the contiguous urbanized portions of Orleans, Jefferson, and St. Bernard parishes lie at or above sea level," with the more densely populated areas generally on higher ground. The average elevation of the city is currently between 1 and 2 feet (0.30 and 0.61 m) below sea level, with some portions of the city as high as 20 feet (6 m) at the base of the river levee in [Uptown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uptown,_New_Orleans) and others as low as 7 feet (2 m) below sea level in the farthest reaches of [Eastern New Orleans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_New_Orleans).[^cite_note-106][^cite_note-107] A study published by the [ASCE](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Society_of_Civil_Engineers) *[Journal of Hydrologic Engineering](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Journal_of_Hydrologic_Engineering)* in 2016, however, stated:

> ...most of New Orleans proper—about 65%—is at or below mean sea level, as defined by the average elevation of Lake Pontchartrain[^cite_note-108]

The magnitude of subsidence potentially caused by the draining of natural marsh in the New Orleans area and southeast Louisiana is a topic of debate. A study published in [*Geology*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geology_\(journal\)) in 2006 by an associate professor at Tulane University claims:

> While erosion and wetland loss are huge problems along Louisiana's coast, the basement 30 feet (9.1 m) to 50 feet (15 m) beneath much of the Mississippi Delta has been highly stable for the past 8,000 years with negligible subsidence rates.[^cite_note-109]

The study noted, however, that the results did not necessarily apply to the Mississippi River Delta, nor the New Orleans metropolitan area proper. On the other hand, a report by the [American Society of Civil Engineers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Society_of_Civil_Engineers) claims that "New Orleans is subsiding (sinking)":[^cite_note-ASCE-110]

> Large portions of Orleans, [St. Bernard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St._Bernard_Parish,_Louisiana), and [Jefferson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jefferson_Parish,_Louisiana) parishes are currently below sea level—and continue to sink. New Orleans is built on thousands of feet of soft sand, silt, and clay. Subsidence, or settling of the ground surface, occurs naturally due to the consolidation and oxidation of organic soils (called "marsh" in New Orleans) and local groundwater pumping. In the past, flooding and deposition of sediments from the Mississippi River counterbalanced the natural subsidence, leaving southeast Louisiana at or [above sea level](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Above_mean_sea_level). However, due to major flood control structures being built upstream on the Mississippi River and levees being built around New Orleans, fresh layers of sediment are not replenishing the ground lost by subsidence.[^cite_note-ASCE-110]

In May 2016, NASA published a study which suggested that most areas were, in fact, experiencing subsidence at a "highly variable rate" which was "generally consistent with, but somewhat higher than, previous studies."[^cite_note-111]

### Cityscape

See also: [Wards of New Orleans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wards_of_New_Orleans) and [Neighborhoods in New Orleans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neighborhoods_in_New_Orleans)



[Bourbon Street](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bourbon_Street), New Orleans, in 2003, looking towards Canal Street



New Orleans contains many distinctive neighborhoods.

The [Central Business District](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Orleans_Central_Business_District) is located immediately north and west of the Mississippi and was historically called the "American Quarter" or "American Sector". It was developed after the heart of French and Spanish settlement. It includes [Lafayette Square](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lafayette_Square,_New_Orleans). Most streets in this area fan out from a central point. Major streets include [Canal Street](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canal_Street,_New_Orleans), Poydras Street, Tulane Avenue and Loyola Avenue. Canal Street divides the traditional "[downtown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Downtown_New_Orleans)" area from the "[uptown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uptown_New_Orleans)" area.

Every street crossing Canal Street between the Mississippi River and [Rampart Street](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rampart_Street), which is the northern edge of the French Quarter, has a different name for the "uptown" and "downtown" portions. For example, [St. Charles Avenue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St._Charles_Avenue), known for its street car line, is called [Royal Street](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Street,_New_Orleans) below Canal Street, though where it traverses the Central Business District between Canal and Lee Circle, it is properly called St. Charles Street.[^cite_note-112] Elsewhere in the city, Canal Street serves as the dividing point between the "South" and "North" portions of various streets. In the local [parlance](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/parlance) *downtown* means "downriver from Canal Street", while *uptown* means "upriver from Canal Street". Downtown neighborhoods include the French Quarter, [Tremé](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trem%C3%A9), the [7th Ward](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/7th_Ward_of_New_Orleans), [Faubourg Marigny](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Faubourg_Marigny), [Bywater](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bywater,_New_Orleans) (the Upper Ninth Ward), and the Lower [Ninth Ward](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ninth_Ward_of_New_Orleans). [Uptown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uptown_New_Orleans) neighborhoods include the Warehouse District, the [Lower Garden District](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lower_Garden_District,_New_Orleans), the [Garden District](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Garden_District,_New_Orleans), the [Irish Channel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irish_Channel,_New_Orleans), the University District, [Carrollton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carrollton,_Louisiana), [Gert Town](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gert_Town,_New_Orleans), [Fontainebleau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fontainebleau,_New_Orleans), and [Broadmoor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Broadmoor,_New_Orleans). However, the Warehouse and the Central Business District are frequently called "Downtown" as a specific region, as in the Downtown Development District.

Other major districts within the city include [Bayou St. John](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bayou_St._John_\(neighborhood\)), [Mid-City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mid-City_New_Orleans), [Gentilly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gentilly,_New_Orleans), [Lakeview](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lakeview,_New_Orleans), Lakefront, [New Orleans East](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_New_Orleans), and [Algiers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Algiers,_New_Orleans).

#### Historic and residential architecture

See also: [Buildings and architecture of New Orleans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buildings_and_architecture_of_New_Orleans)

&#x20;New Orleans is world-famous for its abundance of architectural styles that reflect the city's multicultural heritage. Though New Orleans possesses numerous structures of national architectural significance, it is equally, if not more, revered for its enormous, largely intact (even post-Katrina) historic built environment. Twenty National Register Historic Districts have been established, and fourteen local historic districts aid in preservation. Thirteen of the districts are administered by the New Orleans Historic District Landmarks Commission (HDLC), while one—the French Quarter—is administered by the Vieux Carre Commission (VCC). Additionally, both the [National Park Service](161535.md), via the [National Register of Historic Places](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Register_of_Historic_Places), and the HDLC have landmarked individual buildings, many of which lie outside the boundaries of existing historic districts.[^cite_note-113]

Housing styles include the [shotgun house](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shotgun_house) and the [bungalow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bungalow) style. Creole cottages and townhouses, notable for their large courtyards and intricate iron balconies, line the streets of the French Quarter. American townhouses, double-gallery houses, and Raised Center-Hall Cottages are notable. [St. Charles Avenue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St._Charles_Avenue) is famed for its large [antebellum homes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antebellum_architecture). Its mansions are in various styles, such as [Greek Revival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_Revival_architecture), [American Colonial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_colonial_architecture) and the [Victorian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Victorian_architecture) styles of [Queen Anne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Queen_Anne_style_architecture_in_the_United_States) and [Italianate architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Italianate_architecture). New Orleans is also noted for its large, European-style Catholic cemeteries.

#### Tallest buildings

See also: [List of tallest buildings in New Orleans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_tallest_buildings_in_New_Orleans)



Skyline of the [Central Business District](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Orleans_Central_Business_District) of New Orleans

For much of its history, New Orleans's skyline displayed only low- and mid-rise structures. The soft soils are susceptible to subsidence, and there was doubt about the feasibility of constructing high rises. Developments in engineering throughout the 20th century eventually made it possible to build sturdy foundations in the foundations that underlie the structures. In the 1960s, the [World Trade Center New Orleans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2_Canal_Street) and [Plaza Tower](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plaza_Tower_\(New_Orleans\)) demonstrated skyscrapers' viability. [One Shell Square](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/One_Shell_Square) became the city's tallest building in 1972. The oil boom of the 1970s and early 1980s redefined New Orleans's skyline with the development of the Poydras Street corridor. Most are clustered along [Canal Street](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canal_Street,_New_Orleans) and Poydras Street in the Central Business District.

### Climate

See also: [Hurricane preparedness for New Orleans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hurricane_preparedness_for_New_Orleans)



Snow falls on [St. Charles Avenue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St._Charles_Avenue) in December 2008.

The climate of New Orleans is [humid subtropical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Humid_subtropical_climate) ([Köppen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K%C3%B6ppen_climate_classification): *Cfa*), with short, generally mild winters and hot, humid summers; in the 1991–2020 climate normals the USDA hardiness zone is 9b, with the coldest temperature in most years being about 27.6 °F (−2.4 °C). The monthly daily average temperature ranges from 54.3 °F (12.4 °C) in January to 84 °F (28.9 °C) in August. Officially, as measured at New Orleans International Airport, temperature records range from 11 to 105 °F (−12 to 41 °C) [on December 23, 1989](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/December_1989_United_States_cold_wave), and August 27, 2023, respectively; Audubon Park has recorded temperatures ranging from 6 °F (−14 °C) on [February 13, 1899](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Blizzard_of_1899), up to 104 °F (40 °C) on June 24, 2009, and August 28, 2023.[^cite_note-NWS_New_Orleans/Baton_Rouge,_LA_(LIX)-114] Dewpoints in the summer months (June–August) are relatively high, ranging from 71.1 to 73.4 °F (21.7 to 23.0 °C).[^cite_note-WMO_1961–90_KMSY-115]

The average precipitation is 62.5 inches (1,590 mm) annually; the summer months are the wettest, while October is the driest month.[^cite_note-NWS_New_Orleans/Baton_Rouge,_LA_(LIX)-114] Precipitation in winter usually accompanies the passing of a cold front. There are a median of over 80 days of 90 °F (32 °C)+ highs, 9 days per winter where the high does not exceed 50 °F (10 °C), and less than 8 nights with freezing lows annually, although it is not uncommon for entire winter seasons to pass with no freezing temperatures at all, such as the 2003–04 winter, the 2012–13 winter, the 2015–16 winter and the consecutive winters of 2018–19 and 2019–20. It is rare for the temperature to reach 20 or 100 °F (−7 or 38 °C), with the last occurrence of each being January 17, 2018, and August 27, 2023, respectively.[^cite_note-NWS_New_Orleans/Baton_Rouge,_LA_(LIX)-114][^cite_note-116]

&#x20;New Orleans experiences snowfall only on rare occasions. The [New Year's Eve 1963 snowstorm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Year's_Eve_1963_snowstorm) affected New Orleans and brought 4.5 inches (11 cm). Snow fell again on December 22, 1989, during the [December 1989 United States cold wave](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/December_1989_United_States_cold_wave), when most of the city received 1–2 inches (2.5–5.1 cm). A small amount of snow fell during the [2004 Christmas Eve Snowstorm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2004_Christmas_Eve_Snowstorm) and again on [Christmas](6237.md) (December 25) when a combination of rain, sleet, and snow fell on the city, leaving some bridges icy. The last significant snowfall in New Orleans was on [January 21, 2025](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/January_20%E2%80%9322,_2025_Gulf_Coast_blizzard), when the city received 8–10 inches (20–25 cm) of snow.[^cite_note-117]

### Threat from tropical cyclones



Hurricanes of Category 3 or greater passing within 100 miles, from 1852 to 2005 ([NOAA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NOAA))

[Hurricanes](8282374.md) pose a severe threat to the area, and the city is particularly at risk due to its low elevation, the city being surrounded by water from the north, east, and south, and Louisiana's sinking coast.[^cite_note-Katrina-127] According to the [Federal Emergency Management Agency](73797.md), New Orleans is the nation's most vulnerable city to hurricanes.[^cite_note-128] Indeed, portions of [Greater New Orleans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Orleans_metropolitan_area) have been flooded by the [Grand Isle Hurricane of 1909](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grand_Isle_Hurricane_of_1909),[^cite_note-ReferenceB-129] the [New Orleans Hurricane of 1915](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Orleans_Hurricane_of_1915),[^cite_note-ReferenceB-129] [1947 Fort Lauderdale Hurricane](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1947_Fort_Lauderdale_Hurricane),[^cite_note-ReferenceB-129] [Hurricane Flossy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hurricane_Flossy)[^cite_note-130] in 1956, [Hurricane Betsy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hurricane_Betsy) in 1965, [Hurricane Georges](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hurricane_Georges) in 1998, Hurricanes [Katrina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hurricane_Katrina) and [Rita](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hurricane_Rita) in 2005, [Hurricane Gustav](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hurricane_Gustav) in 2008, [Hurricane Isaac](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hurricane_Isaac_\(2012\)) in 2012, [Hurricane Zeta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hurricane_Zeta) in 2020 (Zeta was also the most intense hurricane to pass over New Orleans) and [Hurricane Ida](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hurricane_Ida) in 2021. The flooding from Betsy was significant and in a few neighborhoods severe, and that from Katrina was disastrous for the majority of the city.[^cite_note-131][^cite_note-132][^cite_note-133]

On August 29, 2005, storm surge from Hurricane Katrina caused catastrophic failure of the [federally designed and built](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flood_Control_Act_of_1965) levees, flooding 80% of the city.[^cite_note-134][^cite_note-135] A report by the American Society of Civil Engineers says that "had the levees and floodwalls not failed and had the pump stations operated, nearly two-thirds of the deaths would not have occurred".[^cite_note-ASCE-110]

New Orleans has always had to consider the risk of hurricanes, but the risks are dramatically greater today due to coastal erosion from human interference.[^cite_note-barry-136] Since the beginning of the 20th century, it has been estimated that Louisiana has lost 2,000 square miles (5,000 km<sup>2</sup>) of coast (including many of its barrier islands), which once protected New Orleans against storm surge. Following Hurricane Katrina, the Army Corps of Engineers has instituted massive levee repair and hurricane protection measures to protect the city.

In 2006, Louisiana voters overwhelmingly adopted an amendment to the state's constitution to dedicate all revenues from off-shore drilling to restore Louisiana's eroding coast line.[^cite_note-137] U.S. Congress has allocated \$7 billion to bolster New Orleans's flood protection.[^cite_note-138]

According to a study by the [National Academy of Engineering](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Academy_of_Engineering) and the [National Research Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_National_Research_Council), levees and floodwalls surrounding New Orleans—no matter how large or sturdy—cannot provide absolute protection against overtopping or failure in extreme events. Rather, the city's levees and floodwalls should be viewed as a way to reduce risks from hurricanes and storm surges instead of eliminating those risks. For structures in hazardous areas and residents who do not relocate, the committee recommended major [floodproofing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flood_mitigation#Protection_of_individual_properties) measures (such as elevating the first floor of buildings to at least the 100-year flood level).[^cite_note-139] Due to rising sea levels, most of New Orleans is at risk of being permanently underwater by the 2050s.[^cite_note-140]

Source: U.S. Decennial Census[^cite_note-DecennialCensus-141] Historical Population Figures[^cite_note-New_Orleans_2010-142][^cite_note-2014CensusEst-143][^cite_note-144][^cite_note-PopEstCities-145][^cite_note-DinHarkins1996-146]  1790–1960[^cite_note-147] 1900–1990[^cite_note-148] 1990–2000[^cite_note-149] 2010–2013[^cite_note-QF-150] 2020 estimate[^cite_note-USCensusEst2020-151] |  |  |

From the [2010 U.S. census](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2010_United_States_census) to 2014 census estimates the city grew by 12%, adding an average of more than 10,000 new residents each year following the official decennial census.[^cite_note-2014CensusEst-143] According to the [2020 United States census](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_United_States_census), there were 383,997 people, 151,753 households, and 69,370 families residing in the city. Prior to 1960, the population of New Orleans steadily increased to a historic 627,525.

Beginning in 1960, the population decreased due to factors such as the cycles of oil production and tourism,[^cite_note-Hollander_2008-152][^cite_note-Frey_1987-153] and as [suburbanization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suburbanization), diversity, and [white flight](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_flight) increased (as with many U.S. cities),[^cite_note-Fussell_2007-154] and jobs migrated to surrounding parishes.[^cite_note-Katz_2006-155][^cite_note-\:6-156] This economic and population decline resulted in high levels of poverty in the city; in 1960 it had the fifth-highest poverty rate of all U.S. cities,[^cite_note-Spain_1979-157] and was almost twice the national average in 2005, at 24.5%.[^cite_note-Fussell_2007-154] New Orleans experienced an increase in [residential segregation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Residential_segregation) from 1900 to 1980, leaving the disproportionately Black and African American poor in older, low-lying locations.[^cite_note-Katz_2006-155][^cite_note-\:6-156] These areas were especially susceptible to flood and storm damage.[^cite_note-Kates_2006-158]

The last population estimate before Hurricane Katrina was 454,865, as of July 1, 2005.[^cite_note-159] A population analysis released in August 2007 estimated the population to be 273,000, 60% of the pre-Katrina population and an increase of about 50,000 since July 2006.[^cite_note-160] A September 2007 report by The Greater New Orleans Community Data Center, which tracks population based on U.S. Postal Service figures, found that in August 2007, just over 137,000 households received mail. That compares with about 198,000 households in July 2005, representing about 70% of pre-Katrina population.[^cite_note-161] In 2010, the U.S. Census Bureau revised upward its 2008 population estimate for the city, to 336,644 inhabitants.[^cite_note-New_Orleans_2010-142] Estimates from 2010 showed that neighborhoods that did not flood were near or even greater than 100% of their pre-Katrina populations.[^cite_note-ReferenceC-162]

Katrina displaced 800,000 people, contributing significantly to the decline.[^cite_note-Nelson_2011-163] Black and African Americans, renters, the elderly, and people with low income were disproportionately affected by Katrina, compared to affluent and White residents.[^cite_note-Nelson_2007-164][^cite_note-Morse_2008-165] Those same groups also had the slowest growth rate in the city after Katrina primarily due to the rising cost of living, low performing public schools, and high crime in lower income neighborhoods.[^cite_note-166][^cite_note-167][^cite_note-168] In Katrina's aftermath, city government commissioned groups such as Bring New Orleans Back Commission, the New Orleans Neighborhood Rebuilding Plan, the Unified New Orleans Plan, and the Office of Recovery Management to contribute to plans addressing depopulation. Their ideas included shrinking the city's [footprint](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ecological_footprint) from before the storm, incorporating community voices into development plans, and creating [green spaces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urban_open_space),[^cite_note-Nelson_2007-164] some of which incited controversy.[^cite_note-Olshanski_2008-169][^cite_note-Reardon_2008-170]

A 2006 study by researchers at [Tulane University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tulane_University) and the [University of California, Berkeley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_California,_Berkeley), determined that as many as 10,000 to 14,000 [undocumented immigrants](7124412.md), many from [Mexico](3966054.md), resided in New Orleans.[^cite_note-undoc-171] In 2016, the [Pew Research Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pew_Research_Center) estimated at least 35,000 undocumented immigrants lived in New Orleans and its metropolitan area.[^cite_note-172] The [New Orleans Police Department](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Orleans_Police_Department) began a new policy to "no longer cooperate with federal immigration enforcement" beginning on February 28, 2016.[^cite_note-173]

As of 2010, 90.3% of residents age five and older spoke English at home as a [primary language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Primary_language), while 4.8% spoke Spanish, 1.9% Vietnamese, and 1.1% spoke French. In total, 9.7% population age five and older spoke a [mother language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mother_language) other than English.[^cite_note-174]
