The city of New Orleans is a political subdivision of the U.S. state of Louisiana. The city and the [parish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_parishes_in_Louisiana) of Orleans operate as a merged [city-parish government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Consolidated_city%E2%80%93county).[^cite_note-GR6-253] The original city was composed of what are now the 1st to 9th wards. In 1836, in response to tensions between the city's old-line Creole population and American newcomers, the state modified New Orleans's charter, dividing the city into three municipalities with local control but still answering to a single mayor and council. In 1852, a new charter was issued, recombining the three municipalities and annexing the city of [Lafayette](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Faubourg_Lafayette),[^cite_note-254] including the Garden District, which formed the 10th and 11th wards. In 1870, Jefferson City, including Faubourg Bouligny and much of the Audubon and University areas, was annexed as the 12th, 13th, and 14th wards. [Algiers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Algiers,_New_Orleans), on the west bank of the Mississippi, was also annexed in 1870, becoming the 15th ward.

New Orleans has a [mayor-council government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mayor-council_government), following a [home rule](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Home_rule) charter adopted in 1954, as later amended. The [city council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/City_council) consists of seven members, five elected from [single-member districts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Single-member_districts) and two members elected [at-large](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/At-large), that is, across the city-parish. [LaToya Cantrell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LaToya_Cantrell) assumed the mayor's office in 2018 as the first female mayor of the city. An ordinance in 2006 established an [Office of Inspector General](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Orleans_Office_of_Inspector_General) to review city government activities.

New Orleans's government is largely centralized in the city council and mayor's office, but it maintains earlier systems from when various sections of the city managed their affairs separately. For example, New Orleans had seven elected tax assessors, each with their own staff, representing various districts of the city, rather than one centralized office. A constitutional amendment passed on November 7, 2006, consolidated the seven assessors into one in 2010.[^cite_note-255]

The City of New Orleans, used [Archon Information Systems](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Archon_Information_Systems) software and services to host multiple online tax sales. The first tax sale was held after Hurricane Katrina.[^cite_note-256] The New Orleans government operates both a [fire department](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Orleans_Fire_Department) and the [New Orleans Emergency Medical Services](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Orleans_Emergency_Medical_Services).

New Orleans is the only city in Louisiana that refuses to pay court-ordered judgments when it loses a case that were awarded to the other party.[^cite_note-257] The city uses a provision in the Louisiana Constitution that prohibits the seizure of a city's property to pay a judgment when it loses a lawsuit. According to an article, "The constitution says the funds can't be seized and can only be paid out if the government appropriates the money. In other words, if the City of New Orleans doesn't budget the funds for judgments, no judge can force the city to pay."[^cite_note-258]

Only if the city council chooses to vote to pay a judgment can the other party be paid. Since the city cannot be forced to pay judgments unless it chooses to do so, it simply does not pay. More than \$36 million in over 500 unpaid judgments issued against the city are simply ignored, some going as far back as 1996.[^cite_note-259]

The Orleans Parish Civil Sheriff's Office [serves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Service_of_process) papers involving lawsuits, provides court security, and operates the city's correctional facilities, including [Orleans Parish Prison](40287582.md). The sheriff's office shares legal jurisdiction with the [New Orleans Police Department](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Orleans_Police_Department) and provides it with backup on an as-needed basis. Before 2010, New Orleans (and all other parishes in Louisiana) had separate criminal and civil sheriff's offices, corresponding to the separate criminal and civil courts: these were merged in 2010.[^cite_note-260] As of 2024 the sheriff is [Susan Hutson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Susan_Hutson), who defeated 17-year incumbent Marlin Gusman in the 2021 New Orleans City Election.[^cite_note-261][^cite_note-262]

## Crime

See also: [New Orleans Police Department](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Orleans_Police_Department) and [Culture of New Orleans § Crime](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_New_Orleans#Crime)

New Orleans is considered one of the most dangerous cities in the nation.[^cite_note-263] As in comparable U.S. cities, the incidence of homicide and other violent crimes is usually highly concentrated in certain lower income neighborhoods.[^cite_note-ritea-264] Arrested offenders in New Orleans are almost exclusively black males from [lower income neighborhoods](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Impoverished): in 2011, 97% were black and 95% were male; 91% of victims were black as well.[^cite_note-265]

The city's murder rate has been historically high and consistently among the highest rates nationwide since the 1970s. From 1994 to 2013, New Orleans was the country's "Murder Capital", annually averaging over 200 murders.[^cite_note-slightly_fewer-266] The first record was broken in 1979 when the city reached 242 homicides.[^cite_note-slightly_fewer-266] The record was broken again reaching 250 by 1989 to 345 by the end of 1991.[^cite_note-267][^cite_note-268] By 1993, New Orleans had 395 murders: 80.5 for every 100,000 residents.[^cite_note-269]

In 1994, the city was officially named the "Murder Capital of America", hitting a historic peak of 424 murders. The murder count was one of the highest in the world and surpassed that of such cities as [Gary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gary,_Indiana), Indiana, [Washington, D.C.](108956.md), and [Baltimore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baltimore).[^cite_note-270][^cite_note-271][^cite_note-nbcnews.com-272][^cite_note-273] In 1999, the city's murder rate dropped down to a low of 158 and climbed to the high 200s in the early 2000s. Between 2000 and 2004, New Orleans had the highest homicide rate per capita of any city in the U.S., with 59 people killed per year per 100,000 citizens.[^cite_note-274][^cite_note-275][^cite_note-276][^cite_note-nbcnews.com-272]

In 2006, with nearly half the population gone and widespread disruption and dislocation because of deaths and refugee relocations from [Hurricane Katrina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hurricane_Katrina), the city hit another record of homicides. It was ranked as the most dangerous city in the country.[^cite_note-277][^cite_note-278] By 2009, there was a 17% decrease in violent crime, a decrease seen in other cities across the country. But the homicide rate remained among the highest[^cite_note-279] in the United States, at between 55 and 64 per 100,000 residents.[^cite_note-280] In 2010, New Orleans's homicide rate dropped to 49.1 per 100,000, but increased again in 2012, to 53.2,[^cite_note-281] the highest rate among cities of 250,000 population or larger.[^cite_note-282]

The violent crime rate is a key issue in every modern mayoral race. In January 2007, several thousand New Orleans residents marched to City Hall for a rally demanding police and city leaders tackle the crime problem. Then-Mayor [Ray Nagin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ray_Nagin) said he was "totally and solely focused" on addressing the problem. Later, the city implemented checkpoints during late night hours in problem areas.[^cite_note-283] The murder rate climbed 14% in 2011 to 57.88 per 100,000[^cite_note-284] rising to No. 21 in the world.[^cite_note-285] In 2016, according to annual crime statistics released by the New Orleans Police Department (NOPD), 176 were murdered.[^cite_note-286][^cite_note-287]

In 2017, New Orleans had the highest rate of gun violence, surpassing the more populated [Chicago](6886.md) and [Detroit](8687.md).[^cite_note-288][^cite_note-289] In 2020, murders increased 68% from 2019 with a total of 202 murders. Criminal justice observers blamed impacts from [COVID-19](63030231.md) and changes in police strategies for the uptick.[^cite_note-290][^cite_note-291] In 2022, New Orleans's homicide rate skyrocketed, leading every major city, hence the city again being declared as the "Murder Capital of America". The 2022 city homicide count increased to 280 which was a 26-year high.[^cite_note-292][^cite_note-293] The NOPD dropped to under 1,000 officers in 2022 which meant the department was severely understaffed for the city's population.[^cite_note-294] NOPD is actively working to reduce violent crime by offering attractive incentives to recruit and retain more officers.[^cite_note-295]

By mid-2025 a prolonged focus on addressing its root causes and reforming the local criminal justice system has reduced the incidence of violent crime to its lowest levels since the early 1970s.[^cite_note-296][^cite_note-297]

## Education

### Colleges and universities



Gibson Hall at Tulane University

New Orleans has the highest concentration of colleges and universities in Louisiana and one of the highest in the Southern United States. New Orleans also has the third highest concentration of [historically black collegiate institutions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/HBCU) in the U.S.



The University of New Orleans



Xavier University of Louisiana

Colleges and universities based within the city include:

- [Tulane University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tulane_University)

- [Loyola University New Orleans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Loyola_University_New_Orleans)

- [University of New Orleans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_New_Orleans)

- [Xavier University of Louisiana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xavier_University_of_Louisiana)

- [Southern University at New Orleans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_University_at_New_Orleans)

- [Dillard University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dillard_University)

- [Louisiana State University Health Sciences Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LSU_Health_Sciences_Center_New_Orleans)

- [University of Holy Cross](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Holy_Cross)

- [Notre Dame Seminary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Notre_Dame_Seminary)

- [New Orleans Baptist Theological Seminary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Orleans_Baptist_Theological_Seminary)

- [Delgado Community College](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delgado_Community_College)

- [William Carey College School of Nursing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Carey_College#School_of_Nursing)

### Primary and secondary schools

See also: [List of schools in New Orleans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_schools_in_New_Orleans)

[Orleans Parish School Board](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orleans_Parish_School_Board) (OPSB), also known as New Orleans Public Schools (NOPS), is the public school district for the entire city.[^cite_note-298] Katrina was a watershed moment for the school system. Pre-Katrina, NOPS was one of the area's largest systems, along with the [Jefferson Parish public school system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jefferson_Parish_Public_Schools). It was also the lowest-performing school district in Louisiana. According to researchers [Carl L. Bankston](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carl_L._Bankston) and Stephen J. Caldas, only 12 of the 103 public schools within the city limits showed reasonably good performance.[^cite_note-299]

Following Hurricane Katrina, the state of Louisiana took over most of the schools within the system, all schools that matched a nominal "worst-performing" metric. Many of these schools, and others, were subsequently granted operating charters, giving them administrative independence from the Orleans Parish School Board, the [Recovery School District](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Recovery_School_District) or the [Louisiana Board of Elementary and Secondary Education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louisiana_Board_of_Elementary_and_Secondary_Education) (BESE). At the start of the 2014 school year, all public school students in the NOPS system attended these independent [public charter schools](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charter_school), the nation's first to do so.[^cite_note-300]

The charter schools made significant and sustained gains in student achievement, led by outside operators such as [KIPP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Knowledge_Is_Power_Program), the Algiers Charter School Network, and the Capital One–University of New Orleans Charter School Network. An October 2009 assessment demonstrated continued growth in the academic performance of public schools. Considering the scores of all public schools in New Orleans gives an overall school district performance score of 70.6. This score represents a 24% improvement over an equivalent pre-Katrina (2004) metric, when a district score of 56.9 was posted.[^cite_note-301] Notably, this score of 70.6 approaches the score (78.4) posted in 2009 by the adjacent, suburban Jefferson Parish public school system, though that system's performance score is itself below the state average of 91.[^cite_note-302]

One particular change was that parents could [choose which school to enroll their children in](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/School_choice#United_States), rather than attending the school nearest them.[^cite_note-The_Times-Picayune_2009-303]

### Libraries

Academic and [public libraries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_library) as well as archives in New Orleans include [Monroe Library](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Loyola_University_New_Orleans#Monroe_Library) at Loyola University, Howard-Tilton Memorial Library at [Tulane University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tulane_University),[^cite_note-304] the Law Library of Louisiana,[^cite_note-305] and the [Earl K. Long](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Earl_Long) Library at the University of New Orleans.[^cite_note-306]

The [New Orleans Public Library](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Orleans_Public_Library) operates in 13 locations.[^cite_note-307] The main library includes a Louisiana Division that houses city archives and special collections.[^cite_note-308]

Other research archives are located at [the Historic New Orleans Collection](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Historic_New_Orleans_Collection)[^cite_note-309] and the [Old U.S. Mint](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Orleans_Mint).[^cite_note-310]

An independently operated lending library called [Iron Rail Book Collective](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iron_Rail_Book_Collective) specializes in radical and hard-to-find books. The library contains over 8,000 titles and is open to the public.

The [Louisiana Historical Association](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louisiana_Historical_Association) was founded in New Orleans in 1889. It operated first at Howard Memorial Library. A separate Memorial Hall for it was later added to Howard Library, designed by New Orleans architect [Thomas Sully](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Sully_\(architect\)).[^cite_note-311]

## Media

Main article: [Media of New Orleans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Media_of_New_Orleans)

See also: [Culture of New Orleans § News & entertainment media](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_New_Orleans#News_&_entertainment_media)

See also: [New Orleans in fiction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Orleans_in_fiction)

Historically, the major newspaper in the area was *The Times-Picayune*. In 2012, the owner [Advance Publications](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Advance_Publications) cut its print schedule to three days each week, instead focusing its efforts on its website, NOLA.com. That action briefly made New Orleans the largest city in the country without a daily newspaper, until the [Baton Rouge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baton_Rouge) newspaper *[The Advocate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Advocate_\(Louisiana\))* began a New Orleans edition in September 2012.&#x20;

In June 2013, the *Times-Picayune* resumed daily printing with a condensed [newsstand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Newsstand) [tabloid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tabloid_\(newspaper_format\)) edition, nicknamed *TP Street*, which is published on the three days each week that its namesake [broadsheet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Broadsheet) edition is not printed. The Picayune has not returned to daily delivery. With the resumption of daily print editions from the *Times-Picayune* and the launch of the New Orleans edition of *The Advocate*, now *The New Orleans Advocate*, the city had two daily newspapers for the first time since the afternoon *States-Item* ceased publication in May 1980. In 2019, the papers merged to form *[The Times-Picayune/The New Orleans Advocate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Times-Picayune/The_New_Orleans_Advocate)*.

In addition to the daily newspaper, weekly publications include *[The Louisiana Weekly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Louisiana_Weekly)* and *[Gambit Weekly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gambit_Weekly)*.[^cite_note-312] Also in wide circulation is the *[Clarion Herald](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clarion_Herald)*, the newspaper of the Roman Catholic Archdiocese of New Orleans.

Greater New Orleans is the 54th largest [designated market area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Media_market) (DMA) in the U.S., serving at least 566,960 homes.[^cite_note-313] Major television network affiliates serving the area include:

- 4 [WWL](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WWL-TV) ([CBS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CBS))

- 6 [WDSU](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WDSU) ([NBC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NBC))

- 8 [WVUE](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WVUE-DT) ([Fox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fox_Broadcasting_Company))

- 12 [WYES](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WYES-TV) ([PBS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PBS))

- 20 [WHNO](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WHNO) ([LeSEA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lester_Sumrall))

- 26 [WGNO](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WGNO) ([ABC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Broadcasting_Company))

- 32 [WLAE](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WLAE-TV) ([Independent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Independent_station_\(North_America\)))

- 38 [WNOL](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WNOL-TV) ([The CW](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_CW_Television_Network))

- 42 [KGLA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/KGLA-DT) ([Telemundo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Telemundo))

- 49 [WPXL](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WPXL) ([Ion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ion_Television))

- 54 [WUPL](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WUPL) ([MyNetworkTV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MyNetworkTV))

[WWOZ](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WWOZ),[^cite_note-314] the New Orleans Jazz and Heritage Station, broadcasts[^cite_note-315] modern and traditional jazz, blues, rhythm and blues, brass band, [gospel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gospel_music), [cajun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cajun_music), [zydeco](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zydeco), Caribbean, Latin, Brazilian, African and bluegrass 24 hours per day.

[WTUL](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WTUL) is Tulane University's radio station.[^cite_note-316] Its programming includes 20th century classical, reggae, jazz, showtunes, indie rock, electronic music, soul/funk, goth, punk, hip-hop, New Orleans music, opera, folk, hardcore, [Americana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Americana_music), country, blues, Latin, cheese, techno, local, world, ska, swing and big band, kids' shows, and news programming. WTUL is listener-supported and non-commercial. The disc jockeys are volunteers, many of them college students.

Louisiana's film and television tax credits spurred growth in the television industry, although to a lesser degree than in the film industry. Many films and advertisements were set there, along with television programs such as *[The Real World: New Orleans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Real_World:_New_Orleans)* in 2000,[^cite_note-Nola.com-317] *[The Real World: Back to New Orleans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Real_World:_Back_to_New_Orleans)* in 2009 and 2010,[^cite_note-318][^cite_note-319] and *[Bad Girls Club: New Orleans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bad_Girls_Club_\(season_7\))* in 2011.[^cite_note-320]

Two [radio stations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radio_broadcasting) that were influential in promoting New Orleans–based bands and singers were 50,000-watt [WNOE](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WLNO_\(New_Orleans\)) (1060) and 10,000-watt [WTIX](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WQNO) (690 AM). These two stations competed head-to-head from the late 1950s to the late 1970s.

## Transportation

### Public transportation

Hurricane Katrina devastated transit service in 2005. The [New Orleans Regional Transit Authority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Orleans_Regional_Transit_Authority) (RTA) was quicker to restore the streetcars to service, while bus service had only been restored to 35% of pre-Katrina levels as recently as the end of 2013. During the same period, streetcars arrived at an average of once every seventeen minutes, compared to bus frequencies of once every thirty-eight minutes. The same priority was demonstrated in RTA's spending, increasing the proportion of its budget devoted to streetcars to more than three times compared to its pre-Katrina budget.[^cite_note-321] Through the end of 2017, counting both streetcar and bus trips, only 51% of service had been restored to pre-Katrina levels.[^cite_note-ridenola-322]

In 2017, the New Orleans Regional Transit Authority began operation on the extension of the Rampart–St. Claude streetcar line. Another change to transit service that year was the re-routing of the 15 Freret and 28 Martin Luther King bus routes to Canal Street. These increased the number of jobs accessible by a thirty-minute walk or transit ride: from 83,722 in 2016 to 89,216 in 2017. This resulted in a regional increase in such job access by more than a full percentage point.[^cite_note-ridenola-322]

#### Streetcars



A New Orleans [streetcar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Streetcars_in_New_Orleans) traveling down Canal Street



A map of the streetcar network

Main article: [Streetcars in New Orleans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Streetcars_in_New_Orleans)

New Orleans has four active [streetcar lines](30733.md):

- The [St. Charles Streetcar Line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St._Charles_Streetcar_Line) is the oldest continuously operating streetcar line in the U.S.

  [^cite_note-323]

  &#x20;The line first operated as local rail service in 1835 between Carrollton and downtown New Orleans. Operated by the Carrollton & New Orleans R.R. Co., the locomotives were then powered by steam engines, and a one-way fare cost 25 cents.

  [^cite_note-324]

  &#x20;Each car is a historic landmark. It runs from Canal Street to the other end of St. Charles Avenue, then turns right into South Carrollton Avenue to its terminal at Carrollton and Claiborne.

- The [Riverfront Streetcar Line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Riverfront_Streetcar_Line) runs parallel to the river from Esplanade Street through the French Quarter to Canal Street to the Convention Center above Julia Street in the Arts District.

- The [Canal Streetcar Line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canal_Streetcar_Line) uses the Riverfront line tracks from the intersection of Canal Street and Poydras Street, down Canal Street, then branches off and ends at the cemeteries at City Park Avenue, with a spur running from the intersection of Canal and Carrollton Avenue to the entrance of City Park at Esplanade, near the entrance to the New Orleans Museum of Art.

- The [Rampart–Loyola Streetcar Line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rampart%E2%80%93Loyola_Streetcar_Line) opened on January 28, 2013, as the *Loyola-UPT Streetcar Line* running along Loyola Avenue from [New Orleans Union Passenger Terminal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Orleans_Union_Passenger_Terminal) to Canal Street, then continuing along Canal Street to the river, and on weekends on the Riverfront line tracks to French Market. The French Quarter Rail Expansion extended the line from the Loyola Avenue/Canal Street intersection along Rampart Street and St. Claude Avenue to Elysian Fields Avenue. It no longer runs along Canal Street to the river, or on weekends on the Riverfront line tracks to French Market.

The city's streetcars were featured in the [Tennessee Williams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tennessee_Williams) play *[A Streetcar Named Desire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A_Streetcar_Named_Desire_\(play\))*. The streetcar line to Desire Street became a bus line in 1948.

#### Buses

[Public transportation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_transport) is operated by the [New Orleans Regional Transit Authority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Orleans_Regional_Transit_Authority) ("RTA"). Many [bus](4146.md) routes connect the city and suburban areas. The RTA lost 200+ buses in the flood. Some of the replacement buses operate on [biodiesel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biodiesel).[^cite_note-325] The [Jefferson Parish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jefferson_Parish,_Louisiana) Department of Transit Administration[^cite_note-326] operates Jefferson Transit, which provides service between the city and its suburbs.[^cite_note-327]

#### Ferries



Ferries connecting New Orleans with [Algiers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Algiers,_New_Orleans) (left) and Gretna (right)

New Orleans has had continuous ferry service since 1827,[^cite_note-328] operating three routes as of 2017. The [Canal Street Ferry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canal_Street_Ferry) (or Algiers Ferry) connects downtown New Orleans at the foot of [Canal Street](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canal_Street,_New_Orleans) with the [National Historic Landmark District](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Historic_Landmark_District) of [Algiers Point](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Algiers_Point) across the Mississippi ("West Bank" in local parlance). It services passenger vehicles, bicycles and pedestrians. This same terminal also serves the Canal Street/Gretna Ferry, connecting [Gretna, Louisiana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gretna,_Louisiana) for pedestrians and bicyclists only. A third auto/bicycle/pedestrian connects [Chalmette, Louisiana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chalmette,_Louisiana) and Lower Algiers.[^cite_note-friendsoftheferry.org-329]

### Bicycling

The city's flat landscape, simple street grid and mild winters facilitate [bicycle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bicycle) ridership, helping to make New Orleans eighth among U.S. cities in its rate of bicycle and [pedestrian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pedestrian) transportation as of 2010,[^cite_note-autogenerated3-330] and sixth in terms of the percentage of bicycling commuters.[^cite_note-autogenerated1-331] New Orleans is located at the start of the [Mississippi River Trail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mississippi_River_Trail), a 3,000-mile (4,800 km) bicycle path that stretches from the city's [Audubon Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Audubon_Park,_New_Orleans) to [Minnesota](19590.md).[^cite_note-332]

Since Katrina, the city has actively sought to promote bicycling by constructing a \$1.5 million bike trail from [Mid-City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mid-City_New_Orleans) to [Lake Pontchartrain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lake_Pontchartrain),[^cite_note-333] and by adding over 37 miles (60 km) of [bicycle lanes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bicycle_lane) to streets, including [St. Charles Avenue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St._Charles_Avenue).[^cite_note-autogenerated3-330] In 2009, [Tulane University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tulane_University) contributed to these efforts by converting the main street through its [Uptown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uptown_New_Orleans) campus, [McAlister Place](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/McAlister_Place,_New_Orleans), into a [pedestrian mall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pedestrian_mall) open to bicycle traffic.[^cite_note-334] The [Lafitte Greenway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lafitte_Greenway) bicycle and pedestrian trail opened in 2015, and is ultimately planned to extend 3.1-mile (5.0 km) from the French Quarter to Lakeview. New Orleans has been recognized for its abundance of uniquely decorated and uniquely designed bicycles.[^cite_note-335]

### Roads

See also: [List of streets of New Orleans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_streets_of_New_Orleans)

New Orleans is served by [Interstate 10](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interstate_10),  [Interstate 610](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interstate_610_\(Louisiana\)) and [Interstate 510](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interstate_510). I-10 travels east–west through the city as the [Pontchartrain Expressway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pontchartrain_Expressway). In [New Orleans East](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_New_Orleans) it is known as the Eastern Expressway. I-610 provides a direct shortcut for traffic passing through New Orleans via I-10, allowing that traffic to bypass I-10's southward curve.

In addition to the interstates, [U.S. 90](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._Route_90) travels through the city, while [U.S. 61](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._Route_61) terminates downtown. In addition, [U.S. 11](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._Route_11) terminates in the eastern portion of the city.

New Orleans is home to many bridges; [Crescent City Connection](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crescent_City_Connection) is perhaps the most notable. It serves as New Orleans's major bridge across the Mississippi, providing a connection between the city's downtown on the eastbank and its westbank suburbs. Other Mississippi crossings are the [Huey P. Long Bridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Huey_P._Long_Bridge_\(Jefferson_Parish\)), carrying U.S. 90 and the [Hale Boggs Memorial Bridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luling_Bridge), carrying [Interstate 310](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interstate_310).

The [Twin Span Bridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/I-10_Twin_Span_Bridge), a five-mile (8 km) [causeway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Causeway) in eastern New Orleans, carries I-10 across Lake Pontchartrain. Also in eastern New Orleans, Interstate 510/[LA 47](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louisiana_Highway_47) travels across the [Intracoastal Waterway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intracoastal_Waterway)/[Mississippi River-Gulf Outlet Canal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mississippi_River-Gulf_Outlet_Canal) via the [Paris Road Bridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Green_Bridge_\(New_Orleans\)), connecting New Orleans East and suburban [Chalmette](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chalmette,_Louisiana).

The tolled [Lake Pontchartrain Causeway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lake_Pontchartrain_Causeway), consisting of two parallel bridges are, at 24 miles (39 km) long, the longest bridges in the world. Built in the 1950s (southbound span) and 1960s (northbound span), the bridges connect New Orleans with its suburbs on the north shore of Lake Pontchartrain via [Metairie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metairie,_Louisiana).

### Taxi service

United Cab is the city's largest taxi service, with a fleet of over 300 cabs.[^cite_note-4WWL-336] It has operated 365 days a year since its establishment in 1938, with the exception of the month after [Hurricane Katrina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hurricane_Katrina), in which operations were temporarily shut down due to disruptions in radio service.[^cite_note-337]

United Cab's fleet was once larger than 450 cabs, but has been reduced in recent years due to competition from services like [Uber](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uber) and [Lyft](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lyft), according to owner Syed Kazmi.[^cite_note-4WWL-336] In January 2016, New Orleans-based sweet shop Sucré approached United Cab with to deliver its [king cakes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/King_cake) locally on-demand. Sucré saw this partnership as a way to alleviate some of the financial pressure being placed on taxi services due to Uber's presence in the city.[^cite_note-338]

### Airports

The metropolitan area is served by the [Louis Armstrong New Orleans International Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louis_Armstrong_New_Orleans_International_Airport), located in the suburb of [Kenner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kenner,_Louisiana). Regional airports include the [Lakefront Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Orleans_Lakefront_Airport), [Naval Air Station Joint Reserve Base New Orleans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Naval_Air_Station_Joint_Reserve_Base_New_Orleans) (Callender Field) in the suburb of Belle Chasse and [Southern Seaplane Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Seaplane_Airport), also located in Belle Chasse. Southern Seaplane has a 3,200-foot (980 m) runway for wheeled planes and a 5,000-foot (1,500 m) water runway for seaplanes.

Armstrong International is the busiest airport in Louisiana and the only to handle scheduled international passenger flights. As of 2018, more than 13 million passengers passed through Armstrong, on nonstops flights from more than 57 destinations, including foreign nonstops from the United Kingdom, Germany, Canada, Mexico, Jamaica and the Dominican Republic.

### Rail

The city is served by [Amtrak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amtrak). The [New Orleans Union Passenger Terminal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Orleans_Union_Passenger_Terminal) is the central rail depot and is served by the *[Crescent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crescent_\(Amtrak\))*, operating between New Orleans and New York City; the *[City of New Orleans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/City_of_New_Orleans_\(train\))*, operating between New Orleans and Chicago and the *[Sunset Limited](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunset_Limited)*, operating between New Orleans and Los Angeles. Up until August 2005 (when [Hurricane Katrina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hurricane_Katrina) struck), the *Sunset Limited's* route continued east to Orlando. The *[Mardi Gras Service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mardi_Gras_Service)* provides service to Mobile following that route.[^cite_note-service_debut_announcement-339]

With the strategic benefits of both the port and its double-track Mississippi River crossings, the city attracted all six of the [Class I railroads](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Class_I_railroad) in North America: [Union Pacific Railroad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Union_Pacific_Railroad), [BNSF Railway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BNSF_Railway), [Norfolk Southern Railway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Norfolk_Southern_Railway), [Canadian Pacific Kansas City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canadian_Pacific_Kansas_City), [CSX Transportation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CSX_Transportation) and [Canadian National Railway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canadian_National_Railway). The [New Orleans Public Belt Railroad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Orleans_Public_Belt_Railroad) provides interchange services between the railroads.

### Modal characteristics

According to the 2016 [American Community Survey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Community_Survey), 67.4% of working city of New Orleans residents commuted by driving alone, 9.7% carpooled, 7.3% used public transportation, and 4.9% walked. About 5% used all other forms of transportation, including taxicab, motorcycle, and bicycle. About 5.7% of working New Orleans residents worked at home.[^cite_note-340]

Many city of New Orleans households own no personal automobiles. In 2015, 18.8% of New Orleans households were without a car, which increased to 20.2% in 2016. The national average was 8.7 percent in 2016. New Orleans averaged 1.26 cars per household in 2016, compared to a national average of 1.8 per household.[^cite_note-341]

New Orleans ranks high among cities in terms of the percentage of working residents who commute by walking or bicycling. In 2013, 5% of working people from New Orleans commuted by walking and 2.8% commuted by cycling. During the same period, New Orleans ranked thirteenth for percentage of workers who commuted by walking or biking among cities not included within the fifty most populous cities. Only nine of the most fifty most populous cities had a higher percentage of commuters who walked or biked than did New Orleans in 2013.[^cite_note-342]

## Notable people

Main article: [List of people from New Orleans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_people_from_New_Orleans)

## Sister cities

[Sister cities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sister_cities) of New Orleans are:[^cite_note-343]

- [Haiti](13373.md) [Cap-Haïtien](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cap-Ha%C3%AFtien), Haiti

  [^cite_note-344]

## Further reading

- Adams, Thomas J., and Steve Striffler (eds.). *Working in the Big Easy: The History and Politics of Labor in New Orleans.* Lafayette, Louisiana: University of Louisiana at Lafayette Press, 2014.

- Berry, Jason. *City of a Million Dreams: A History of New Orleans at Year 300.* Chapel Hill, NC: University of North Carolina Press, 2018.

- Chamberlain, Charles D., *New Orleans: A Concise History of an Exceptional City*, Baton Rouge, Louisiana State University Press, 2025. ISBN 978-0807184875

- Dessens, Nathalie. *Creole City: A Chronicle of Early American New Orleans.* Gainesville, Florida: University Press of Florida, 2015.

- Ermus, Cindy (ed.). *Environmental Disaster in the Gulf South: Two Centuries of Catastrophe, Risk, and Resilience.* Baton Rouge, LA: Louisiana State University Press, 2018.

- Fertel, Rien. *Imagining the Creole City: The Rise of Literary Culture in Nineteenth-Century New Orleans.* Baton Rouge, Louisiana: Louisiana State University Press, 2014.

- Gitlin, Jay (2009). [*The Bourgeois Frontier: French Towns, French Traders, and American Expansion*](https://books.google.com/books?id=x4P2TI_mfsQC\&pg=PA159). Yale University Press. p. 159. [ISBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISBN_\(identifier\)) [978-0-300-15576-1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special\:BookSources/978-0-300-15576-1).

- Holli, Melvin G., and Jones, Peter d'A., eds. *Biographical Dictionary of American Mayors, 1820-1980* (Greenwood Press, 1981) short scholarly biographies each of the city's mayors 1820 to 1980. [online](https://archive.org/details/biographicaldict0000unse_r8s1); see index at p. 409 for list.

- Marler, Scott P. *The Merchants' Capital: New Orleans and the Political Economy of the Nineteenth-Century South.* New York: Cambridge University Press, 2013.

- Powell, Lawrence N. *The Accidental City: Improvising New Orleans.* Cambridge, Massachusetts: Harvard University Press, 2012.

- Simmons, LaKisha Michelle. *Crescent City Girls: The Lives of Young Black Women in Segregated New Orleans.* Chapel Hill, NC: University of North Carolina Press, 2015.

- Solnit, Rebecca, and Rebecca Snedeker, *Unfathomable City: A New Orleans Atlas.* Berkeley, California: University of California Press, 2013.

[^cite_note-:6-156]: &#x20;["Hope in the Midst of Despair – AHA"](https://www.historians.org/perspectives-article/hope-in-the-midst-of-despair/). *American Historical Association*. Retrieved July 26, 2025. When black migrants arrived in New Orleans after World War II and settled into the city's notorious housing projects, shanties, and shotgun houses. The city's white community fled to the surrounding suburbs of Chalmette, Arabi, Mereaux, Metarie, Kenner, Slidell, Mandeville, and Covington. The black presence in the city went from roughly 33% in 1940 to 43% by 1970. White flight out of the city led to disinvestment in economic development, jobs, housing, and education. The city's power structure refused to listen to, let alone address, black issues, placing the city on a racial powder keg by 1960.
